An entry whose period ends after the filing's acceptance date (New York), starts after it ends, or whose value is not finite is withheld and counted (`statement_malformed`); the shares path's end-date cap is not applied, because here the date is the key and a moved date would be a different period.

*Source and re-ingest.* The whole path is behind **`edgar.statement_facts_enabled`, default `false`**: while it is off the ingest never calls `statement_facts`, the adapter makes no request and writes no cache for it, and the backfill, which runs the same `_ingest_filings`, is unchanged; T78 turns it on for the owner's store and flips the default in the same PR once the real-store run has passed. Values come from the companyfacts payloads the shares already use (`{}` and keyless members as #566 and #599), read once per CIK per run (one zip member read serves both the shares and the statement caches) and parsed into a cache beside the shares one (`statement_facts/v<N>/<cik>.json`, keyed by the CIK's latest stamped cover-form accession and the configured tags, so a new filing or a new tag re-parses that CIK). The cache holds the CIK's parsed entries **unstamped, and its conflict entries with them**, so a cache read restores `statement_conflict_keys` exactly as a parse sets it (else a run served from the cache would derive the candidate a conflict withheld, and a re-run would add a row), and every entry is stamped at read time from the stamps file, as the shares are, so a stamp that arrives later with no new cover-form filing is applied on the next read without a re-parse. **Which counts a cache read restores:** a cross-run fetch-pass cache hit (the CIK's cache key unchanged since it was last parsed) sets `statement_conflicts` for that CIK from the cached conflict entries, exactly as a parse would, because the cache carries those entries; it also sets `statement_unstampable` correctly, because stamping (and so settling an accession as unstampable) happens at read time from the stamps file on every read, cache hit or not, never baked into the cache at parse time. It does **not** set `statement_non_usd`, `statement_malformed` or `statement_none` for that CIK: the cache holds only parsed entries and conflict entries, never the parse-time skip counts, and it cannot tell "no tags filed" apart from "every candidate entry skipped, filtered or non-USD" once both look like zero cached entries. Those three counts come from a parse only; a CIK whose fetch-pass read is a cache hit contributes zero to each of them for that run, whatever it contributed when the entries were first parsed. The two filters `_company_facts` applies are carried over unchanged: an accession FSN extracted under another CIK and absent from this CIK's FSN cache (a co-registrant's combined filing), and an accession whose cached cover page names another entity, contribute nothing, so a REIT's operating partnership never stores the parent's revenue as its own. Statement facts are full history every run, as the shares are (`--since` does not limit them). `statement_facts(cik)` is asked once per CIK in the run's **fetch pass** (req 1, #173: every filing question before the write lock), so each CIK's payload read, parse and cache write happens with no store connection open and the adapter's `statement_*` counts and its `statement_conflict_keys` list are plain attributes set then, as the shares path's are, and the counts are taken from this fetch pass only. The statement cache is written for every CIK the pass answered (a member that trails the CIK's latest filing included, keyed by the accession the payload reached so the next run's key mismatches and re-parses it), because under the lock the write phase asks the adapter for one CIK at a time and that call is a **cache-file read, never a request** and never an in-memory memo of the records (the first load's 10^7 records would not fit the way the shares' memo does); that per-CIK read restores `statement_conflict_keys` for the ingest to consult but does not re-run the counting, so the write phase never adds to the run row's `statement_*` counts. Which source the bytes come from: today the bulk zip is used only when the count of stale **shares** caches crosses `edgar.bulk_stamp_threshold_ciks`; that count includes stale statement caches **only while the switch is on** (off, the decision is exactly today's, so the backfill never downloads the bulk zip on their account), and **`--bulk-from-cache`** forces the bulk path outright: `bulk_company_facts` returns `edgar.cache_dir/bulk/companyfacts.zip` as it stands, provided it opens as a zip, without a request, and every CIK whose zip member holds its latest stamped cover-form accession is served from it with no per-CIK request; only a CIK the zip lacks or whose member trails its latest filing goes to the per-CIK API, counted as today. The flag is off by default, and without it the nightly zip is re-downloaded as today. The flag changes where bytes come from, never a `known_at`: every stamp still comes from submissions. The first load on an existing store is therefore one ordinary `tradepartner ingest --source edgar --bulk-from-cache` run with the switch on.

*Ingest.* The fetch pass (the `_build_filings` pass that `_prefetch` freezes, req 1; both its callers run it) asks `statement_facts(cik)` for every issuer CIK before the lock and has the adapter fill each CIK's parsed statement cache; the proxy records only **which CIKs were filled**, never the records (the `_Recorded` memo would otherwise hold the first load's 10^7 records in Python). Inside the transaction the write phase asks the adapter for one CIK at a time, a cache-file read that makes no request (a CIK the pass did not fill raises, as any unanswered question does), and drops that CIK's records after its insert, so the lock is held only while writing and Python holds one CIK at a time. That read restores `statement_conflict_keys` for the derived-candidate rule below but does not re-trigger the adapter's own `statement_conflicts`, `statement_non_usd`, `statement_malformed`, `statement_unstampable` and `statement_none` counts, which the adapter sets once, from the fetch pass only (the run row's remaining counts below — `statement_held`, `statement_restated`, `statement_vintage_late` and `statement_derived` — are the ingest's own, computed here as this same write phase processes each CIK's rows). Per CIK: records in acceptance order, the held and late rules above, insert-or-skip against the keys the store holds for that CIK, with the derived candidate taken at its filing's turn (above). The rows are written **per CIK inside the run's one transaction** through a bulk insert (one frame per CIK registered with DuckDB and inserted in one statement, the tz check done once per frame, never `insert_row` per row), so Python holds one CIK's rows at a time (about 10^3) and the transaction holds the rest (about 10^6 vintages on the first load, 10^7 records read); the commit is the run's, as today. Idempotent: a re-run adds zero rows. The run row counts: added, `statement_held`, `statement_unstampable`, `statement_vintage_late`, `statement_conflicts`, `statement_restated`, `statement_non_usd`, `statement_malformed`, `statement_derived`, and `statement_none` (CIKs with a companyfacts payload and none of the tags, IFRS-only filers included); with `--rebuild-statement-facts`, also the row count deleted.

*Health.* `health.py` and the page report, at the latest T: the share of `universe_as_of(T)` names whose issuer has a `revenue` and a `total_assets` row known at T with `period_end` within `universe.max_shares_age_days` of T (the same freshness bound as rule 7, no new threshold); the derived share of `gross_profit` rows; the last run's counts above (three of them parse-only, amendment #740 below), with a warning while `statement_vintage_late > 0`; the switch's state. Coverage is reported, not thresholded: no rule reads it. **This bound is not any hypothesis's own.** A signal in the `profitability` backtest family (amendment 2026-10-03, #720) reads with its own `profitability.max_fact_age_days` (B3's default 548, 18 months, wider than `universe.max_shares_age_days`'s 400), so this page's coverage share under-reports how much a profitability signal can actually use each February to June, when a filer's prior-year row is between 400 and 548 days old and `universe.max_shares_age_days` has already called it stale. Kept as is, with this note, rather than taking the wider bound, both, or a per-family bound: this page answers one question, "is the shares/facts pipeline healthy", with rule 7's bound, the one every consumer shares; it does not answer "how much can each registered signal use", which varies per hypothesis and belongs in that hypothesis's own file. Rule 7's bound is **not claimed to be right for annual facts** (the amendment itself calls it "wrong for annual facts", "Config keys" above): the coverage share dips every February to June by the filing calendar, not a pipeline fault, so a reader should compare like months, and T78's evidence month-end is better chosen outside that window for the same reason (#728). Integrity rules for `--check`: the four common-column rules; `period_start` NULL exactly when `period_days = 0`, else `period_days = period_end − period_start`; every `derived` row equals the difference of the two stored `revenue` and `cost_of_revenue` rows with its `cik`, `period_end`, `period_days` and `filing_accession`, both present (a missing component row fails the rule); `basis` in `{reported, derived}`.

*Amendment 2026-10-10 (#1034): comparative groups by duration class.* The Periods rule above grouped a filing's columns by exact `period_days`, and T77 built it so (`edgar.parse_statement_facts`, the read-time recompute in `edgar_source`'s statement cache read, and `ingest._derived_candidates`, which groups derived candidates the same way; pinned by `test_comparative_groups_on_exact_period_days_as_the_spec_reads`). Since `period_days = period_end − period_start`, a filing's columns of one kind rarely have equal days: a calendar filer's FY2020 is 365 days and its FY2019 comparative 364 (leap year), a 52/53-week filer's years are 363 and 370, and quarters run 89 to 91 days (97 for a 14-week quarter). On real data the prior columns therefore land in groups of their own and are **not** flagged, so the acceptance clause "a 10-K carrying the fiscal year and two comparative years of `Revenues` ... the two comparatives flagged" holds only when every column has equal days. Nothing stored is wrong except the flag: the first-vintage rule, the key, the hold and late guards and the derived-candidate rule never read `comparative`, and no read path, signal or health rule reads it today (the B3 signal's "latest period wins" picks by `period_end`, [b3-gross-profitability](../hypotheses/b3-gross-profitability.md) point 4). **The rule from this amendment:** `comparative = TRUE` when the row's `period_end` is earlier than the latest `period_end` the same filing carries for the same fact and the same **duration class**, where a duration's class is its length in whole months, `floor(period_days / 30.4375 + 0.5)` (30.4375 = 365.25 / 12, a calendar constant named once in code, not a tunable threshold), and an instant (`period_days = 0`) is class 0. That puts 363 to 370 days at 12, 89 to 97 at 3, 181 to 188 at 6 and 272 to 279 at 9, keeps a two-month 10-KT stub (about 59 days, class 2) apart from a quarter, and is transitive, which a tolerance band is not. The key and `period_days` are unchanged: only the grouping that sets the flag moves. Applies at all three places the rule is computed, in one change; derived candidates group by the same class. Acceptance: the "Comparative by duration class" criterion in the statement-facts list above, at all three places. Existing rows: the statement cache holds entries unstamped and the adapter recomputes `comparative` on every read, so the change needs **no statement-cache version bump** (the cache stores no flag; a bump would force every CIK to re-parse for nothing), and one `ingest --source edgar --rebuild-statement-facts --bulk-from-cache` run after the code lands corrects the stored flags without a re-parse (as any rebuild, it re-derives the whole table, so a late vintage or a newly arrived stamp moves too); since nothing reads the flag, that rebuild can wait for the next one another reason needs, and until then the owner's store carries the old flag (T78's run, PR #1139). Merging this amendment records the owner's choice of option (b), duration class (the PR lists the alternatives); the code is follow-up #1435.

*Amendment 2026-10-10 (#740): three run counts are parse-only.* *Source and re-ingest* above already says which counts a cache read restores: a CIK whose fetch-pass read is a statement-cache hit contributes its `statement_conflicts` and `statement_unstampable` but **zero** to `statement_non_usd`, `statement_malformed` and `statement_none`, because the cache holds no skip counts. So in the *Ingest* run row those three are **parse-only**: they count the CIKs this run parsed (a new cover-form filing, a changed tag or unit list, a missing or unreadable cache file), not every CIK the run answered, and on a steady nightly run, where most CIKs are cache hits, they sit near zero whatever real skips exist. They are not coverage figures. The other seven counts are unchanged in meaning. *Health:* the page shows the last run's counts as before and labels those three "parsed CIKs only (a cache hit adds zero)", so a near-zero figure is not read as current coverage; no new count and no new threshold. The label is part of the health view, so the web app that replaces the Streamlit page under ADR 0018 carries it at parity. *T78 and open questions 5 and 7:* T78 (#1127, PR #1139) was the first run with the switch on, and its auditor evidence ([PR #1139 comment](https://github.com/josejuarez96/tradepartner/pull/1139#issuecomment-6038506732)) shows a statement cache file written from the zip payload for every CIK, so every CIK was parsed and its `statement_none` 954 and `statement_non_usd` 2,359 are full-parse counts; the inputs to open questions 5 and 7 stand. Any later re-count for those questions must come from a run that parses every CIK: one after `edgar.cache_dir/statement_facts/v<N>/` is moved aside, or one under a changed `edgar.statement_tags` or `statement_units` (the cache key carries their digest, so every CIK re-parses) or a new statement cache version. `--rebuild-statement-facts` alone reads the cache and undercounts, so its three counts are not evidence for either question. The page label is a follow-up code change (`dashboard/health_page.py`, #1435); nothing else changes.

**Survivorship gap** at T: let W = the sessions in (previous rebalance session, T]. L = common, non-benchmark listings active at **any** session in W (known at T). M = names in L still listed at T with no bar at T, plus names in L delisted (not transferred) inside W whose last bar is more than `gap.missing_tail_sessions` before the **last session before the Form 25 filing**. Count share = |M|/|L|. Size share = Σ(shares × last known raw close) over M / Σ over L; names with no close ever contribute zero and are counted. Side categories, reported separately and not in M: `unclassifiable`, `truncated_history` (rule 6), `stale_shares` (rule 7).

*Amendment 2026-10-07 (#1199, owner decision 2026-10-07; [ADR 0003 amendment](../decisions/0003-data-adapters-local-first.md#amendment-2026-10-07-1199-the-stale-listing-rule)): stale listings.* A security's current listing still live at T (`listed`, or `transferred` with its end on or after T's session) whose last traded bar known at T (latest revision, positive volume) is before W's first session and more than `gap.stale_listing_sessions` XNYS sessions before T's session is **stale**: for the gap only it counts as ended on the session of that bar, so the security is not in L on its account and never in M; another listing of the security active in W (a Form 25 filed inside W) keeps it in L under the rules above. A name dark more than that many sessions whose last bar is inside W is not stale: live and `no_bar_at_t` as today, whatever N. A live listing with no traded bar at all stays in M as `no_bar_at_t` (ADR open question 2). The securities removed are a fourth side category, `stale_listings` (security id, last bar, sessions dark), reported beside the three above and never in M; a stale security is in none of the other three. `universe_as_of`, the `truncated_tail` test, `gap.missing_tail_sessions`, `gap.count_share_threshold`, the master, the ingest and the backtest's `delisting_exit` and `stale_exit_sessions` are unchanged. The argument, the limits, the consequences, the measured baseline (holdout max 0.122, 33 of 33 rebalances above 0.05, `survivorship_gap` at every month-end of both windows), the spike's estimate (holdout max about 0.017 for scenarios A to D combined, an estimate) and the comparison point for the rule alone (about 0.025 at 2026-09-30, arithmetic on the measured cause table) are in the ADR; the code change, its no-look-ahead test, a sample check of the names removed and the re-measure are step 2 of #1199.

**Amendment 2026-10-04 (#735): price-to-security resolution.** A price source keyed by symbol (Alpaca) maps each `(ticker, session)` to a `security_id` only through the master's `listings` rows known at the run (`ListingResolver`). This mapping through the master is the sanctioned form of req 3's "never resolve by bare ticker". A row that maps to nothing is dropped and counted, never guessed. *Terms.* A security's **span** of a ticker runs from a listing's `valid_from` until that security's next listing with a different ticker (exclusive; open while there is none). Consecutive rows with one ticker, such as a second exchange, are one span. A span is **contested** when a later span of the ticker belongs to another security that traded under a different ticker before (a rename into it: Roundhill's `META` ETF, then Facebook's `FB` -> `META`). Alpaca serves a renamed company's history under the new symbol (#104), so the contested span's rows are not assigned. *Amended 2026-10-14 (#1696, T192): a span is contested only while live at the renamer's span start: its end by these Terms or by rule 7 (never rule 8's hand-over cut) is exclusive, so a span ending on or before the renamer's start session is not contested. Such an ended span is asked with `asof` inside itself and never falls back to a request without `asof` (the renamer's copies), and takes no corporate action, as while contested: actions are fetched without `asof`, so its splits and dividends are not stored (counted on the run row as unresolved action rows); a split inside it shows as a price jump (#787) and its returns carry no dividends. Its first-span lead, if any, keeps its own rules. See item 3 of the 2026-10-14 amendment.* *Amendment #843 (owner decision (d) on #843, option (b)): rename lead.* When a security's equity span under a new ticker directly follows its own assigned span under another ticker (a rename; the old span not ended by rule 7, the new one not contested), the new ticker also resolves to that security on sessions from `alpaca.rename_lead_days` (default 400) before the new span's start, never before the old span's start, while no span resolves the ticker on that session and the old ticker resolves to no other security. Such a row is assigned only when the security has no row (bar or zero-volume placeholder) that session from the rest of the same payload, so the hole from the old symbol's last bar to the first cover page showing the new ticker closes, and no session is stored twice (Alpaca serves the renamed company's history under the new symbol too, #104). Bars keep their session-close `known_at`; the identity comes from the master at the run, as for every row. Corporate actions take no rename lead, and `repair-resolution` judges actions without it (the first-span lead of amendment #974 below is the exception). The reused-ticker variant (PROG/AAN) is left to #869.

*Amendment #974 (owner decision 2026-10-05 on #974, option (a)): first-span lead.* A security's earliest ticker-bearing cover page comes with iXBRL cover tagging (fiscal periods ending after 2019-06-15: July 2019 for large filers, 2020 and 2021 for smaller ones), often years after it started trading, and the companies snapshot back-dates a ticker (`snapshot_static`, req 3) only for a survivor whose earliest cover-page ticker and exchange it still shows. A name renamed since, moved to another exchange, or delisted before the snapshot has no listing before that cover page, so no bar before it: META and Elevance start on 2019-07-24, and 192 names fail universe rule 6 until 2020-07/08, about 19% of each 2019-11 to 2020-06 universe, a selection bias against renamed and dead names. So a security's **first span** also *leads* to the security, under the span's ticker, on the sessions from the security's **first session** (the session of the earliest `known_at` among its `securities` rows, its first filing's acceptance, never a later revision's ingest clock; the `snapshot_static` row's `valid_from` rule) up to the span's start, when all of these hold: the span is assigned (rules 1-4 and 6; its rule-7 end does not matter, a delisted name is the case to fix) and not contested; every earlier span of the security is a placeholder span (one holding no ticker is skipped; a non-equity, same-day, later-class or claim span before it means the earliest ticker evidence is in doubt: no lead); the span starts after the first session; and no other security's span of the ticker, of any kind but placeholder, covers a session of that window (a reuse the master knows of gets no lead, the whole window, and is counted). On each session the lead answers only while `resolve` gives the ticker to no one, and a row is assigned only when the security has no row of its own that session, as for #843. Bars keep their session-close `known_at`; the identity is the master's at the run, the accepted deviation of the "Look-ahead" paragraph below and the same back-dating `snapshot_static` gives a survivor. `universe_as_of(T)` at a T before the cover page's `known_at` still excludes the name under rule 2 (`not_listed`, the #35 decision), so the lead admits no name to any universe before its listing is known; from then on rule 6 reads the lead bars, every one closed before T. Unlike #843, **corporate actions take the first-span lead** (an action resolves on the session before its ex-date; there is no second symbol here to serve it twice), so a split inside the window adjusts the bars it belongs to instead of leaving an unexplained jump for #787 to exclude the name on; `repair-resolution` judges actions with the first-span lead and never with the rename lead. The backfill fetches a led security in the months its lead covers (`--since` bounds the fetch as for every month, so the lead reaches the backfill start at most) and never counts it in the staleness denominator there (no listing is known in the month); `--fill-holes` lists and fetches its empty months and the partial month at the window's end as it does a rename gap (#891). The run row counts the securities led and those refused for a reuse (rule 5). `alpaca.first_span_lead` (default `true`) turns it off. Accepted limits, shared with `snapshot_static` spans: the master knows no pre-2019 holder absent from today's snapshot (a Form 25 names a class and exchange, never a ticker), so a ticker reused after an unlisted company's delisting can serve that company's bars under the lead (BTU and SD are the static-span cases), and the first-session floor keeps them off only when the security's earliest filing is near its first trade (a CIK that filed years before it listed leads to the backfill start anyway); the #787 gate (`traded_only`, jumps) covers these at read time and #797 is the follow-up for the rest. A `<cik>:<class>` id's first session is its class row's `known_at`, as for its static row, so a second class leads only from its first cover page. In a daily run a new name's lead window is a hole until `--fill-holes`, as #869 item 2 for renames. Not chosen: pre-2019 tickers from another source. EDGAR submissions carry current tickers only, like the snapshot; a historical ticker file is a new source whose rows would be `snapshot_static`-like and equally invisible before their fetch (#35), so it fixes no universe the lead does not; pre-2019 `dei:TradingSymbol` facts in the FSN text files have unverified coverage and would need a research brief (open question 1).

**Amendment 2026-10-10 (#1408, owner decision on #987 item 1).** The #974 first-span lead's first session is the earliest `known_at` among a security's non-retracted `securities` rows known at the run. A row marked `retracted = TRUE` cannot move the floor earlier; an earlier live row remains eligible even when a later retraction corrected its key. This changes nothing on any store the code writes today: `store.retraction.retraction(row, at)` always stamps the retraction after the row it withdraws, so a retracted row is never a security's earliest `known_at`; the rule guards the floor against a back-dated retraction should one ever be written, and #987 item 1's risk (an earlier *withdrawn* row pulling the floor earlier) stays as the #974 amendment accepted it.

The rules (owner-approved on #735):

1. **Only equity classes hold tickers.**
   - A row is left out when its ticker field names no ticker. That means `PLACEHOLDER_TICKERS` in `adapters/alpaca_prices.py` (`''`, `N/A`, `NA`, `None`, `Not applicable`, `Trading Symbol`), compared case-insensitively after trimming spaces, quotes and brackets at both ends, or any field with no letter (`-`, `0`). The words in `PLACEHOLDER_WORDS` (`true`, `false`, `yes`, `no`) count only when not in capitals, so TrueCar's `TRUE` resolves. `NA` stays on the list although Nano Labs trades as `NA`: other filers write it for "not applicable", and a later such row would take Nano Labs' bars. Nano Labs is therefore left out and counted.
   - A row is also left out when its class is not equity, read from the row's own `class_title` by `store.classify.listing_kind`. The rule runs on the title's head: up to the first comma, less a trailing "included as part of the units" clause, and less a shareholder-rights plan attached to a common class, as in "Common Shares (including Rights under Shareholder Rights Plan)". The kinds, in order:
     - `coupon`: a `%` in the head, or anywhere in a title whose head is not a common class.
     - `preferred`, `debt` (notes, debentures, bonds), `warrant`, `right`: the head names the word.
     - `preferred`: a depositary *share* that is not American or Global, so a foreign issuer's ADS titled only "Depositary Shares" is left out too.
     - `unit`: a unit, unless common or depositary units or partner interests (MLP units).
     - A row with no title (`None` or blank: a snapshot) takes the ticker-suffix rule of classification rule 7.
   - Everything else is equity, including ADSs and unrecognised share titles. This is wider than the universe's `security_types` on purpose, since the universe filters again.
   - The rule applies **per span**: a span with any non-equity row is left out whole.
   - A left-out row still ends its security's previous span. A non-equity span holds no ticker but still **shadows** another company's span that started on or before it, from its own start and while it lasts: the ticker resolves to nothing there rather than going back to the older company. Spans of the same company are not shadowed, so a company's notes listed under its common ticker never take the ticker or hide the common, whatever their start. Placeholder spans shadow nothing.
   - *Amendment #844 (unreadable tickers).* A footnote marker after a symbol (trailing `*`, a bracketed number, or a bracketed exchange name) is that symbol (`VAL*`, `UPH(1)`, `BAX (NYSE)` -> `BAX`), in what is sent to Alpaca too; any other bracketed group (`HEI (A)`, `GOOG (Class C)`) names a class and stays no symbol. An equity row whose ticker is still no Alpaca symbol (`New York Stock Exchange` on three of Vulcan's 10-Qs, `F&G`, `PTN1`) is a cover page's slip and never ends its security's span: it is dropped on a day that also lists a readable equity ticker of the security, and otherwise read as the ticker of the security's last row before that day when that row is a readable equity row. As a security's first row, or after a placeholder or non-equity row, it stays a span of its own, never sent. A Form 25 on a row read this way ends the span under rule 7 as if the row had named the held ticker. Accepted cost: a ticker change whose new ticker is written unreadably keeps the old ticker on the security until its next readable row (rule 6 still stops a reuse claimant once the holder has left). The run row counts these rows.
2. **One security, two tickers on one day** (two different tickers on equity rows of one day). If one of them is the ticker of the security's row just before that day (an equity row), the other rows of that day are a typo and are dropped: the security keeps its ticker (#819; FutureFuel listing Ford's `F` beside its own `FF` keeps `FF` and leaves `F` to Ford). Otherwise the security holds neither ticker, nor any later one, from that day. Its rows are not assigned, and the tickers resolve as if it had never listed them. Placeholder and non-equity rows do not count toward the pair.
3. **One company, two classes, one ticker (silent takeover).** Within one CIK (ids `<cik>` and `<cik>:<class>`), the class that held a ticker first keeps it. A span of another class of the company that starts while the first class holds the ticker never holds it, not even after the first class moves on: a Class B listed under the Class A's ticker never takes the Class A's prices. Such a span shadows other companies like a left-out span (rule 1). Across companies the most recent span still wins (ticker reuse).
4. **Same-start ambiguity.** Two securities' spans of one ticker starting on the same day are **ambiguous**: the ticker resolves to nothing while they are the latest, like a contested span, and the run continues. *Amendment #874:* a repair successor (`<cik>@<date>`, #820) whose span starts on the same day as a span of its own company (`<cik>` or `<cik>:<class>`) is no tie: that row is the predecessor's copy of the successor's row (#826), so the predecessor's span is dropped before rules 3 and 6 (it is never a tie nor a rival claim) and the successor holds the ticker. A later successor of an older successor of the company (a second relisting) is judged the same way. The contested rule still sees the dropped span, so a rename into the ticker still contests an older company's span. The run row counts the dropped spans.
5. **Counted.** Every alpaca run row's message carries the resolver's counts: placeholder-ticker listings, the rows of non-equity spans by kind, same-day-pair listings and securities, unreadable-ticker listings ignored (#844), predecessor spans duplicated by a successor (#874), spans ended at a stopped line (#847), later-class, ambiguous and contested spans, and the co-registrant and disputed claims of rule 6 (held until the holder leaves), and (#974) the securities with a first-span lead and those refused one for a reuse. It also carries the bar and action rows of the chunk that resolved to nothing.
6. **Registrant check (#793).** The ticker belongs to the registrant whose equity trades under it. Across companies, a span that starts while another company's span of the ticker holds it (the *holder*: an assigned span under rules 1-4) is a **claim**, unless the holder **left** on or before the claimant's start. The evidence is read from the store as known at the run:
   - A holder *left* on the earliest of two days. One is the `effective_on` (else the day after `end_session`) of a listing of the holder's own security that `listing_ends_as_of` calls `delisted`, on or after the holder's span start, with an equity class by `listing_kind` (a transfer is not leaving, nor is a preferred or note class). The other is the day after its company's last cover-page `shares_outstanding` filing (UTC day of `known_at`), once more than `alpaca.registrant_quiet_days` (default 180) have passed by the run with none. Since 2022 a company's share-count filings are 91 days apart at the median and 168 at the 99th percentile, so 180 days marks a missed filing. The leaving day depends only on the holder's own filings and the run's day, never on which of the two companies filed last.
   - A claim is a **co-registrant** claim if the holder has not left at all and both companies reported one share count for one date no more than `alpaca.registrant_quiet_days` before the claimant's start, or later. That is the combined filing behind the claimant's listing, as with AEP Texas or a REIT's operating partnership citing the parent's stock; on the 2026-10-04 store the shared date is 0-30 days before the start. The holder keeps the ticker. The claimant never holds it, not even after the holder's span ends, and shadows nothing.
   - Any other claim is **disputed**. The ticker resolves to nothing from the claimant's start until the earlier of the claimant's own span end and the *wait's end*: the earlier of the holder's span end (any next listing row of the holder's security, rule 1 included) and its leaving. From the wait's end the claimant holds the ticker as an ordinary span, unless another security's span of the ticker starts that same day (no rule-4 tie). With several holders the claim waits for the last of them. Examples: MG&E citing MGE Energy's `MGEE`, where nothing in the store shows which equity trades, and an early successor listing in the window before its predecessor goes quiet.
   - A holder whose common was delisted or that went quiet before the claimant's start loses the ticker to the newer span as before. So a holding-company successor (Xerox Holdings 2019, NorthWestern Energy Group 2023) takes it from its first listing, while a reorganization younger than the quiet bound, with no delisted listing, is disputed until its predecessor goes quiet.
   - With no evidence on the holder (no share count and no delisted listing), the newer span wins as under rules 1-4.
   - Known limits, both falling back to newer-wins or holder-keeps (#799). `listing_ends_as_of` cannot tell a secondary exchange's Form 25 from leaving, so such a holder counts as left. In a holding-company reorganization with no Form 25, where the predecessor keeps filing and cites the successor's share count, the predecessor would keep the ticker as a co-registrant. Neither occurs on the 2026-10-04 store, where all five co-registrant verdicts are a subsidiary or operating partnership.
   - Accepted, counted cost: a claim whose holder renamed but whose new ticker is missing from the master stays disputed while that holder files. On the 2026-10-04 store that is five new SPACs (ACAC, CLBR, GIG, GPAC, KCAC); #799 tracks the missing rows.
7. **A span ends at its own delisting (#819).** A span ends on the `effective_on` (else the day after `end_session`; never before the day after the Form 25's acceptance) of a listing of its security under its ticker that `listing_ends_as_of` calls `delisted`, with an equity class, when that listing is the span's last row. If the span still holds the ticker after rules 3 and 6, the ticker resolves to nothing from that day until another span starts: it never falls back to another company's span that the ended span had superseded, while a span that started after the ended one (a successor listed before the old common's Form 25 took effect) keeps it. A span that never held the ticker (later-class, co-registrant) shadows nothing by ending. A reused ticker therefore never prices a delisted security (Eagle Bulk carrying another equity's `EGLE` from 2025). A delisted listing followed by a row of the same ticker for the same security (a cover page filed years after an old Form 25, as for Citigroup's `C`; a relisting; a transfer outside the window; a reorganized security's new row, #820) does not end the span: the security went on trading under it, and its bars before that row stay assigned. *Amendment #847:* unless the line had stopped trading before that row: more than `master.transfer_window_sessions` XNYS sessions strictly between the delisted listing's `end_session` (its last bar before the security's next row) and the later row's `valid_from`. Then the span ends on the delisting's day as if the listing were its last row (Sotheby's, private from October 2019, filed a cover page naming `BID` for its debt in November 2019 and so kept another issuer's 2026 `BID` bars). With no `end_session` (no bar known), the later row keeps the span as before. Accepted cost: a bar gap cannot tell a real relisting after months off-exchange (no Alpaca bars there) from such a cover page, so a relisting under the same security and ticker more than that window after its last bar is cut too. The run row counts the spans ended this way, and `repair-resolution --dry-run` lists their later bars for the owner to review before deleting (#830's protected ids among them mean stop). *Amendment #943:* a line whose `end_session` is on or after the delisting's effective day traded through that delisting, so it never stopped at it, whatever gap in the stored bars follows: the later row keeps the span (Seagate's untitled 2002 `STX` row, ended by its 2010 Cayman-to-Ireland Form 25, had bars from 2016 and a store hole to 2019, and would have lost its whole 2016-2026 history). And `alpaca.accepted_relistings` (default empty) lists security ids the owner accepted as genuine long-gap relistings (MiMedx, Nasdaq to OTC in 2019 and back in November 2020): the stopped-line cut is never applied to their spans, which run on as before #847; the run row counts the spans kept this way. A malformed id (not a ten-digit CIK, a `<cik>:<class>` or a `<cik>@<day>` successor) refuses the config, so every command fails rather than cut a listed name silently. Not done, for want of evidence that tells a 1:1 successor from a reuse: a predecessor keeping a successor CIK's bars until the successor's own listing (BG, FERG, NWE, ZYME, MTAL). Those sessions are unassigned; rule 8 (#1314) now acts on these five where their `asof` bars stop (see its last bullet). Known limit, as for rule 6: `listing_ends_as_of` cannot tell a secondary exchange's Form 25 from leaving, so when the secondary listing is the span's last row the span ends while the security still trades (none on the 2026-10-04 store). The run row counts the ended spans, the spans kept through a delisting by a later row of the ticker, and the dropped typo rows.
8. **A span ends when another issuer takes its ticker (#1314 item 2, span lag).** A span's end (its security's next listing row, rule 7) comes from the security's next cover page, weeks or months after the ticker really passed on. Until the other issuer's own span starts, the old span holds the ticker, and ingest filed the new holder's bars under the old security. On the 2026-10-07 store that hit 7 securities (TOPW 163 sessions; LHC, AAN, CLBR, GIG, KCAC, HYAC 8 to 64 each; the read-only check on #1314). The rule:
   - **Terms.** For an assigned equity span of security S under ticker T, **D** is the start of the first span of T of another company (`_company` differs; a non-placeholder span of any kind) that starts after S's span starts and before S's span's current end (open if none). **L** is S's last stored bar (an Alpaca source, latest revision known at the run, volume > 0) on a session before D on which S's T-span covers S. `prices_daily` keys bars by security, not ticker, so a bar counts for this span only on the span's own sessions.
   - **When.** All of these must hold:
     - D exists.
     - Under rule 6 applied without rule 8, D's span is an ordinary newer span, not a co-registrant or disputed claim against S. So the rule never hands a claimant the ticker of a holder that has not left.
     - L exists.
     - More than `master.transfer_window_sessions` XNYS sessions lie strictly between L and D. This is #847's stopped-line test.

     Then S's span ends on the earlier of its current end and the session after L (the **hand-over day** H). Otherwise it is unchanged. The 7 cases have 8 to 163 wrong sessions, all over the default of 5. Of them, TOPW, LHC and HYAC are fixed by this rule once item 3 has run. CLBR, GIG and KCAC are fixed only where rule 6 no longer calls them disputed (#799). AAN waits on #869 (see Not covered).
   - **Evidence and item 1's `asof` contract.** The hand-over is read from S's own bars, never from the other issuer's.
     - Since #1314 item 1, ingest asks for bars under T without `asof` only for the security holding T's latest span when that span has no end. Every other security is asked with Alpaca's `asof` set `alpaca.asof_offset_days` (default 7) after the start of its first span of T, kept before that span's end and before the start of another company's next span of T.
     - When that request is empty, one more `asof` request runs from the security's first day under T to the window's end. Only if that one is empty too does the request go out without `asof`. That last fallback can store the new holder's bars under S again on S's sessions, so L stays late and the rule leaves S alone, as in the AAN case. The run row names every symbol asked without `asof`, so such a span is visible.
     - An `asof` near the span's start names S's company, so S's stored bars stop when it stopped trading as T: it renamed, de-SPAC'd, or was delisted with its Form 25 or cover page late.
     - No filing or listing row marks the hand-over day itself. Cover pages lag it, which is the defect. A Form 25 names no ticker. 8-K cover pages and Alpaca `name_changes` are #869's undecided sources.
     - H is #847's `end_session` idea applied across companies: a stored bar is evidence known at the run.
   - **After H.** From H the ticker resolves to nobody until D, as after a rule-7 end, and S's span does not fall back to an older company. Amendment #974 refuses a first-span lead when another security's span covers a session of the lead window. For the first span of a new issuer whose window meets a span ended by rule 8, both the refusal test and the lead window run over `[max(first session, H), D)` only, never over the earlier sessions S held. So when T is the new issuer's first span, its lead can fill `[max(first session, H), D)` with the new holder's bars under the other #974 conditions, and the refusal count says which test refused a lead. Any other span gets nothing there, and those sessions stay unassigned and counted. S's own bars before H are untouched, and by construction S has no bar on or after H before D.
   - **Store holes.** A hole in S's stored bars right before D (#831) looks the same as a stopped line. `--fill-holes` (#876) therefore lists S's months in `[H, D)` as holes of S, as if rule 8 had not cut, and refetches them with item 1's `asof` request for S.
     - **Where the bars land.** For that refetch, a bar returned by S's request on a session of `[H, D)` is assigned to S as if rule 8 had not cut, never to the new issuer's lead or to anyone else. The next run recomputes L and H from it, so the cut is never self-confirming.
     - **A line that really stopped.** The refetch returns nothing, so the months are listed again on every `--fill-holes` run. Each such run counts them as `rule8_window` holes with nothing stored, and costs one `asof` request per month.
     - **Accepted cost.** Until that run, a hole longer than the window before D reads as a stop, and S looks delisted at H.
   - **Order.** The rule needs S's stored bars to be its own, and ingest withdraws nothing (req 9). A re-ingest alone therefore leaves the new holder's bars stored under S up to D, L stays the session before D, and nothing changes (fail-safe). #1314 item 3, the owner's repair, must first **delete** S's Alpaca bars and actions, all revisions, on the sessions where item 1's `asof` series does not serve S's company (the mismatched sessions of the read-only check on #1314), then refetch with item 1's requests. The rule takes effect at the first run after that.
   - **Forward runs.** Until D's listing is known, S's span is T's latest open span, so a daily run asks for T without `asof` and stores the new holder's bars under S. Once D is known those bars keep L at the session before D, and the rule stays inert for S. This is the live-lag limit of #869 item 2. It needs a repair like item 3, or #869's dated evidence, and is an accepted cost.
   - **Counted.** The run row counts the spans ended this way. `repair-resolution --dry-run` lists S's bars on sessions from H that the resolver no longer assigns (none after item 3, by construction). The new issuer's newly led sessions come from a backfill or `--fill-holes`, as for #974.
   - **Not covered.** If Alpaca serves the new holder even with `asof` inside S's span, the stored bars never stop, so L gives nothing. This is PROG Holdings' `AAN`: the spun-off Aaron's Company took `AAN` on 2020-12-01, the day PROG became `PRG`, and Alpaca maps `AAN` to it for every `asof`. That needs dated ticker-change evidence (#869: (a) 8-K cover pages or (c) Alpaca `name_changes`). When #869 lands, H is the earlier of the bar-based day and #869's dated change. A later holder that is not in the master has no D, and no rule applies: item 1 already leaves S's sessions after its last `asof` bar empty.
   - **Interaction with rule 7's "Not done" cases, #1118 and #828.**
     - **Rule 7's five** (BG, FERG, NWE, ZYME, MTAL: a predecessor keeping a successor CIK's bars until the successor's own row) are cross-company, so rule 8 applies to them. Where Alpaca maps the predecessor's `asof` to one continuing entity, its bars run on to D and nothing changes. Where they stop more than the window before D, the predecessor's span ends at H. The dry run lists these for the owner's review before any delete.
     - **#1118 and #828 (relisting and successor rules).** Within one company nothing is cut, because `_company` folds `<cik>:<class>` and `<cik>@<date>` ids. A relisting, a #1118 item 5 class row (Valaris), a #820 successor, a #828 false successor on a transfer and a #943 accepted relisting are never "another issuer". If #1118's rules move a row to a different CIK, rule 8 then applies to it like any other company's span.

*Repair (#819).* `tradepartner repair-resolution [--dry-run]` deletes every Alpaca bar and action, all revisions, whose key the resolver built at the run does not assign (a bar's session, an action's session before its ex-date), in one write transaction, and records an `ingestion_runs` row (source `alpaca`, mode `repair`, status `repaired`, never `ok`). A real run takes the dry run's bar and action row counts (`--expect-bar-rows`, `--expect-action-rows`) and deletes nothing on any other count; both list every security found with its counts. Deleting is the remedy, not a revision row: such a row was never its security's fact, so no `known_at` makes it right, and a revision would leave it visible before the repair. As with the `statement_facts` rebuild, past as-of reads change; a trial logged before the repair is reproducible only from its own export. Bars the corrected rules newly assign come only from a backfill with a new `--since`.

*Look-ahead.* The resolver is a key mapping built from every listing known at the run, not at each session, so a backfill maps 2016 bars with listings filed later; rule 6 likewise reads the share counts and listing ends known at the run, and the run's day. The bars' own `known_at` is unchanged. Rules 2-4 and the shadowing act only from the triggering span's start; rule 7 only from the effective day, after the Form 25's acceptance, except that a later row of the ticker keeps sessions before its own `valid_from`. Four rules can change an earlier session's mapping: a contested span, a span made non-equity by a later row of the same ticker (rule 1, per span), rule 6, whose verdict depends on filings after the claimant's start, and rule 8 (#1314), which frees sessions `[H, D)` once another company's later listing (D) is known, reading stored bars known at the run as #847 does; it moves a span's end earlier only onto sessions where the security had no bar, and the freed sessions go to nobody or to the new issuer's #974 lead. A fifth is the first-span lead (amendment #974): it assigns sessions before any `valid_from` of the security from filings known at the run. The security's first ticker-bearing cover page adds them (at the run that first knows it, as a `snapshot_static` row's `valid_from` does), and another security's span of the ticker inside the window, first known at a later run, takes them all away again at that run, where `repair-resolution` then lists and deletes the bars; a new placeholder row before the span changes nothing, any other new earlier row of the security removes them too. The first two normally make the session *unassigned*. Under rule 6 a claimant gains sessions only from the holder's leaving day on, never before it. A holder's leaving after the claimant's start ends a co-registrant verdict, so its sessions from the claimant's start become disputed (unassigned). A span re-started at a claim's end can newly contest an older span (or tie with one, rule 4). A holder that goes quiet after a run hands the sessions after its last filing to the claimant at a later run. A holder's fresh filing after a quiet spell turns the claim back into a wait. In one edge, a span removed by the first two stops making a sibling class later-class (rule 3) or stops contesting an older span, and a rule-6 claim stops contesting one; that other span then resolves. Which names have prices at an early T can therefore depend on later filings. This is an accepted deviation, bounded and visible through the run-row counts.

**Amendment 2026-10-04 (#787): price-quality gate.** Before about 2019 a listing is `snapshot_static` (today's ticker back-dated), and a price source keyed by symbol can serve another equity's bars under it: a flat price on zero volume, then a one-day move of +1,216% (BTU) or +12,900% (SD) that 12-1 momentum would rank first. The gate acts at read time, so nothing is re-ingested.

*(1) A zero-volume bar is missing, not a price.* `prices_as_of` and `adjusted_prices_as_of` take `traded_only` (default `false`). With `true`, a bar whose latest revision known at T has volume 0 is left out; adjustment factors still use every stored bar, and a later revision with volume brings the bar back. `universe_as_of` reads with it, so such a bar is no close for rules 4 and 8, no dollar volume for rule 5, and a missing bar for rule 6. The backtest provider reads its frames with it (backtest spec req 1). The survivorship gap still reads every stored bar, unchanged: it measures whether the source served bars at all, and #801 weighs applying the gate there. Execution's own reads are also unchanged.

*(2) An unexplained jump is reviewed.* `price_jumps_as_of(T, ids=None, settings)` lists, per security, each traded bar known at T where:
- its raw close over the previous traded bar's close is above `universe.max_jump_ratio` (2.5) or below `universe.min_jump_ratio` (0.4); and
- the splits and dividends known at T, with an ex-date after the previous bar and on or before this one, do not bring `(close + dividends) × split ratios / previous close` back inside those bounds.

`accepted` is true when `universe.accepted_price_jumps` names the jump as `"<security_id>@<session>"`.

ADR 0006 rule 6 also fails a name with an unaccepted jump on a session in its `min_history_months` window, with reason `price_jump`. That is the issue's "for that span": once the jump leaves the window, or the owner accepts it, the name is judged on its bars again. `price_jump` is not a missing-data reason, so the gap's truncated-history category does not count it.

The gate removes names from selection only:
- **Held names stay marked through a jump.** Hiding a real crash from a held name would flatter results, so a held name is marked on its bars.
- **The signal's coverage depends on the window.** A signal whose lookback is longer than `universe.min_history_months` can read across a jump once the name is readmitted. 12-1 momentum, with 12 months for both, is covered; a longer lookback is not.
- **Exclusions are not counted per rebalance.** The universe's exclusions name them, and the owner empties the review list before registering.

`health` lists every jump known at T, accepted or not, as the owner's review list. It is not an integrity rule. `--jumps-before DATE` lists only jumps before a hypothesis's `holdout.start`.

*Frozen parameters.* The three keys sit in `universe.*`, so a hypothesis freezes them (backtest spec req 10). Three consequences follow:
- Every hypothesis registered before this change must be registered again.
- An acceptance made after registration does not reach that hypothesis. Accepting later means a new hypothesis.
- A jump is accepted on data validity alone: do the bars belong to this issuer's own trading? Never on backtest results or on what the name returned afterwards.

`traded_only=True` is code, not a parameter, so a trial carries it only through its code version.

*Point in time.* The list at T reads only rows known at T, so a split first known after T leaves its jump on the list at T. A later zero-volume revision of a bar that a held position was marked at is a retracted mark, and that backtest step fails (backtest req 2's valuation rule); it is never silently re-marked.

A `snapshot_static` span trusted only from its first continuous traded bar (the AA case) is #797.

**Amendment 2026-10-04 (#845): shares plausibility.** Filers sometimes tag `EntityCommonStockSharesOutstanding` at the wrong scale: ×1,000 or ×1,000,000 ([research #174](../research/2026-09-25-cover-page-facts-at-scale.md) F22). AJG, SKY, VCEL and CCRN then ranked among the largest companies at some rebalances. The check runs at read time, and nothing is re-ingested (owner decision (A) on #845).

*The check.* `universe.shares_as_of(T, ids, settings)` walks each security's shares facts known at T in `as_of_date` order. Each fact gets the value rule 7 would read for its date, and a date with two class rows is skipped.
- The first fact is accepted. A value of zero or less is never a share count: it is always rejected, is never a baseline and cannot be accepted.
- Each later fact is compared with the **last accepted** fact, moved by every split known at T with an ex-date after that fact's `as_of_date` and on or before this one's.
- A fact is out of line when that ratio is above `universe.max_shares_ratio` (100) or below its inverse. An out-of-line fact is rejected unless `universe.accepted_shares_facts` names it as `"<security_id>@<as_of_date>"`. An accepted fact becomes the new baseline.

Comparing with the last *accepted* fact, not the previous one, also rejects a second bad quarter in a row (AJG 2020-03 and 2020-06).

*Use.* Rules 7 and 8 use the last accepted fact. Its age is judged by `universe.max_shares_age_days`, so a run of rejections ends in `stale_shares`, which is missing data that the gap counts. `Universe.shares_fallbacks` lists, per rebalance, each class whose latest fact was rejected and the fact used instead. The survivorship gap's size share reads the same selection.

*Review.* `health` lists every out-of-line fact known at T, accepted or not, as the owner's review list (`shares_outliers`). `--jumps-before DATE` keeps only facts dated before DATE. As with the jump list, the keys sit in `universe.*`:
- A hypothesis freezes them, so every hypothesis registered before this change must be registered again.
- A fact is accepted on data validity alone, never on results.

*Point in time.* Only facts and splits known at T are read. A correcting filing known after T never changes the pick at T, and a split first known after T leaves a split-explained move rejected until it is known.

*Accepted risk.* A genuine move of more than 100× in reported shares is rejected until the owner accepts it, for at most `max_shares_age_days`. (The #853 amendment below now uses such a move once its run has spanned `max_shares_age_days`.) Such moves are rare: a large reverse split is explained by its split action, and only a de-SPAC or a huge issuance would trigger it. A switch between an undimensioned total and a class row on consecutive dates is compared as is.

*When the first fact is the bad one* (possibly WMG), there is nothing earlier to compare with. The first fact becomes the baseline, and the correct later facts show on the review list as out of line. The owner then accepts the **first correct fact** in `universe.accepted_shares_facts`. Each later fact is compared with it, so one entry repairs the series. (Superseded for the common case by the #853 amendment below, which re-anchors on its own once the correct facts have held for `max_shares_age_days`.)

**Amendment 2026-10-05 (#853): re-anchoring a contradicted shares baseline.** Under #845 the first positive shares fact is the baseline. When it is mis-scaled, every correct later fact is out of line with it and is rejected. The bad fact then ages past `universe.max_shares_age_days`, and the name fails rule 7 with `stale_shares` at every later rebalance. The H1 trial-1 audit (#853) found this. A read-only probe of the owner's store (facts known at close 2023-12-29) shows three forms:
- **One bad first fact.** EOG (2.52e11 on 2009-08-03, about 1,000× too large), Truist (6.48e14), West Pharma (3.33e13), Sempra (2.46e14), Eaton, Walgreens and Corteva (100 shares), TechnipFMC (1 share).
- **A short bad run.** ICE (1 share, twice), Linde (25,000 shares, twice, from the shell company before the merger), Amcor (13,000).
- **A long run that is real.** Ally (1.33e6 shares while private, then 4.8e8 from 2014-03-31, after a pre-IPO split that has no split action). WMG (about 1,060 shares of the subsidiary filer until 2020-01-31, then 8.86e7).

Owner decision 2026-10-05 on #853: option (a), an automatic point-in-time re-anchor, not entries in `universe.accepted_shares_facts`. The rule was chosen on data validity: the audit's exclusions, plus membership and cap checks under H1's frozen settings. It was not chosen on trial 1's returns.

*The rule.* The #845 walk is unchanged, with one addition.
- **The run.** The walk tracks the current run of rejected facts. Each fact in the run is in line with the one before it, within `universe.max_shares_ratio` either way, after the splits known at T with an ex-date after the earlier fact's `as_of_date` and on or before the later one's. A rejected fact that is out of line with the previous fact in the run starts a new run. An accepted fact ends the run, whether it was in line, owner-accepted or re-anchored.
- **The re-anchor.** A rejected fact is **re-anchored** (accepted, and made the new baseline) when its `as_of_date` is more than `universe.max_shares_age_days` after the `as_of_date` of the first fact of its run.

In words: the new scale has held, unbroken, for longer than rule 7 trusts any single fact. Skipped facts neither extend nor break a run. Those are dates with two class rows and values that are not a share count. A value that is zero, negative, NaN **or infinite** is not a share count: it is always rejected and never a baseline. This closes the +inf hole that quant-auditor pass 3 on #849 found.

*Use and review.* Rules 7 and 8, the gap's size share and `shares_fallbacks` read a re-anchored fact like any other accepted fact. `SharesPick.outliers` and health's `shares_outliers` gain a `reanchored` column. A re-anchored fact is listed with `reanchored = true` and `accepted = false`, and it stays in `pending`, like every fact the owner has not accepted. So `tradepartner health` prints it, and the owner sees each re-anchor and both scales. Accepting it in `universe.accepted_shares_facts` clears it from the list, at the usual cost: the frozen set changes. The rejected facts before it stay listed, as now.

*Why these limits, and why no new key.* Both limits are existing frozen keys, used in their existing sense:
- `max_shares_ratio` decides whether two facts agree.
- `max_shares_age_days` is how long rule 7 relies on one fact.

A scale that every filing has confirmed for longer than that is no longer one bad fact. It is the filer's reported series. A transient error never qualifies: two bad quarters (AJG 2020-03 and 2020-06), or bad facts interleaved with in-line ones (CCRN 2020-2022), keep resetting the run. A filing gap does not qualify either: the run measures its own span, not the time since the baseline. The rule therefore adds no threshold, and CLAUDE.md's "thresholds and limits come from config" holds with no new `universe.*` key. The precedent is `traded_only=True` above, a rule that is code, not a parameter, and reaches a trial through its code version. A hypothesis's frozen set and hash do not change (H1 is registered at params sha256 `6a1d884e…`). A run after this change is a new trial of the same hypothesis, with the new `code_version` on its row, and every earlier trial still counts toward N. One consequence: an owner who changes `max_shares_age_days` also moves the re-anchor point.

*Considered and rejected.*
- **Re-anchor once the baseline is stale and two rejected facts agree** (the first draft of this amendment). A filing gap longer than the age limit, followed by two bad facts, re-anchors at once onto the bad scale (spec-critic on PR #995).
- **"N consecutive agreeing facts", with N as a new key.**
  - A new `universe.*` key changes the frozen key set, so `load_frozen` refuses every registered hypothesis until it is registered again. T96's `FROZEN_KEY_DEFAULTS` would not help: an old registration would read the key's default, and only an "off" default reproduces the old behaviour.
  - It is also less safe. On the same probe, N = 3 re-anchors CCRN, a #845 case, onto its bad ×1,000 scale in 2021, and CCRN sits in the top 1,000 at a cap of about $1T at 15 rebalances. N = 2 puts names above $3.2T at 6 member-rebalances.
- **Owner entries** (`universe.accepted_shares_facts`, option (b) on #853). They change the frozen set too, and need one entry per name.

*Measured effect.* The rule was prototyped outside the tree and run with H1's frozen settings at the 41 rebalances from 2020-08-31 to 2023-12-29, against the owner's store opened read-only.
- **Every re-anchor.** Over the full history of the securities that are members or rule-7 exclusions at any of those rebalances, there are 34 re-anchors (one per security). In 23 the old baseline is below 100,000 shares: a shell, subsidiary or pre-merger placeholder. In 8 it is above 5e9 shares, a scale error such as EOG or Truist. The other 3 (Ally among them) are pre-IPO counts. All 34 move to the scale of the filer's later reports. The 11 dated inside the window are DXC (2018-10-31), REKR, RBBN, AMRX, CLBK, Linde (2020-01-31), Corteva (2020-10-29), Amcor (2020-11-04), WMG (2021-11-23), Viatris (2022-04-25) and EMBC (2023-08-02), each from a baseline of 10 to 25,000 shares.
- **Members.** 13 to 17 change per rebalance (median 16), out of about 1,005. 20 companies enter:
  - at all 41 rebalances: ICE, EOG, DXC, West, Ally, Ensign, Linde, Qorvo, Eaton, Truist;
  - at fewer: Corteva and Amcor (38), Walgreens (36), TechnipFMC (28), WMG (26), Viatris (20), TripAdvisor (15), VEREIT (15), Sanmina (7), E*TRADE (2).

  They displace marginal names near the cap cut: 348 distinct names, most at 1 to 3 rebalances. A name that is a member both before and after the change keeps its cap at every rebalance. The largest member cap at any rebalance is $3.09T.
- **Rule-7 exclusions.** 48 → 28 at 2020-08-31 and 76 → 52 at 2023-12-29. The rest are not this bug (next paragraph).

*Not this bug* (#991). Dick's and PBF have no shares fact. Simon Property, Fox, Vertiv, Watsco and Lithia have facts that stop years before the window, or that moved to a class id. Schwab and McCormick have two class rows on the latest date. The re-anchor does not touch them.

*Point in time.* Only facts and splits known at T are read, as under #845. The rule compares the `as_of_date`s of facts known at T, never T itself or a later filing. So a fact first known after T cannot re-anchor at T, and the pick at T is a function of the rows known at T alone.

*Accepted risks.*
- **A filer at the wrong scale for longer than the age limit, in every filing.** The bad scale then becomes the baseline, the corrected facts form a new run, and they wait out the limit again. The owner sees both re-anchors in `shares_outliers`. None occurs among the 34 above.
- **A genuine move of more than 100×** (a pre-IPO split with no split action, a de-SPAC). It is used once its run has spanned `max_shares_age_days`, and until then the name is `stale_shares` or on a fallback. This replaces the #845 accepted risk above ("rejected until the owner accepts it"): the owner can still accept earlier, at the cost of a new frozen set.
- **A newly listed name with a bad first fact.** It waits for its correct facts to span the age limit, roughly five quarters. In H1's window, Corteva misses the first 3 rebalances, Amcor the first 3, and Viatris enters from 2022-04.

**Amendment 2026-10-04 (#859): retracting a false master row.** `securities` and `listings` are append-only, and ingest writes only rows that change an as-of read, so a key the builder stops producing is never touched. When a master rule is corrected, the row the old rule wrote stays live: #826 created a false successor for WillScot, `0001647088@2020-08-10`; #835's rules no longer make it, yet the owner's store still lists it, and WSC's post-2020-08 bars sit under that id (owner decision on #859: options a, x, one PR).

*Retraction (schema version 11).* Both tables gain `retracted BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT FALSE`, the pattern of `corporate_actions.cancelled` (#108). A retraction is the stored row again, with its `source` and `provenance` kept, `retracted = TRUE` and `known_at = ingested_at =` the correcting run's clock, read once the write lock is held and never back-dated. Like a #108 cancel, a `filing` or `snapshot_static` retraction row therefore carries a run-clock `known_at`, not an acceptance or fetch time. As-of reads take the latest revision per key first, then drop a retracted one, and return frames shaped as before (no `retracted` column). So master reads at T before the correction return what they did, and reads after it do not. `retracted` is one of the key's value columns for ingest, so a key a later build derives again is written back live, stamped at that run. The benchmark identity reads (#840) skip retracted rows. The migration rebuilds the two tables on every store without the column, every row kept with `retracted = FALSE`. A read-only connection to a version-10 store still opens and reads every master row as live (it has no retraction), until a writing command migrates it (`ingest`, `paper start`, or `master-retract --apply`).

*What it gives up.* The correction is known only from the correcting run. Every as-of read at a T between the false row's `known_at` and that run still returns it, next to the corrected rows #835 stamps at their filings' acceptance. For WSC, from 2020-08-10 two securities list the ticker. Which one a session's bars resolve to follows the #735 rules, so a backtest over that window can see both ids, and once `repair-resolution` deletes the false id's bars, the survivorship gap and the universe treat that id as a name with no bars. T45b's exact WSC history comes from `repair-resolution` (#819) moving the bars at "now", not from this retraction. Master reads reproduce. A trial that priced bars `repair-resolution` later deletes reproduces only from its own export, as for the #660 rebuild.

*Underived.* A stored row is **underived** when all of these hold at the check:
- it is live (the latest revision, not retracted);
- its `source` is `edgar` and its `provenance` is `filing`;
- its key is not among the build's rows. The key is `security_id`, or `(security_id, ticker, exchange, valid_from)` for listings;
- its CIK (the first ten characters of its id) had no filing fail or get quarantined in this run's fetch pass.

Only `filing` rows are judged, because they come from full-history inputs (the filing index, cover pages, Forms 25). A `snapshot` or `snapshot_static` row comes from the companies snapshot, which lists only names trading today. A delisted or renamed name's snapshot row is legitimately absent from today's build, and retracting it would erase delisted history. Such rows are never judged; a false one needs another remedy. Nor is a successor id (`<cik>@<valid_from>`, #820) with a stored non-`filing` listing, or any of its rows: one relisted on snapshot evidence has a `filing` securities row, yet exists only while the snapshot names it. A key the build still derives with other values (a reworded title) is a revision ingest writes. A config-seeded benchmark is never judged.

*Who finds and who retracts.*
- **Every EDGAR ingest chunk** records its underived set in `master_underived`, under its run row's id and clock. **The latest check at T** is the EDGAR run row with status `ok` or `retracted` that finished last by T. `health --check` fails `underived_master_rows` while that check's rows are still live at T. Health stays store-only. Until the first EDGAR ingest on version 11, the latest check is an older run that recorded nothing, so the rule passes. The owner's first step after this change is that ingest.
- **`tradepartner master-retract`** is owner-run (option x; never automatic). It builds the master exactly as an EDGAR ingest would and lists every underived row, marking `[traded]` any security a journal order names. It prints the CIKs it did not judge and a 12-character digest of the set (table, key, stored `known_at`), and changes nothing by default.
- **`--apply --expect-rows N --expect-digest D`** takes the dry run's count and digest, and refuses on any other set, writing nothing. It also refuses a `[traded]` row (a security any journal order, adjustment, daily position or lot names, in any window) unless the owner adds `--allow-traded`: a window holding the name could lose its price or fall back to an older ticker. Otherwise, in one transaction, it writes the retractions, a run row (source `edgar`, mode `retract`, status `retracted`, never `ok`; health's last-ingest view skips it) and the retracted rows in `master_underived`.
- **It refuses, judging nothing,** when the fetch pass fails, when a failure names no CIK (an FSN extraction failure), when the build derives no security, and on a dry run against a store below version 11.

*Owner-kept successors (amendment 2026-10-05, #922).* `master.keep_successors` (default empty) lists successor ids (`<cik>@<YYYY-MM-DD>`, with `-<n>` for a second one that day, #820) the owner accepted although the current rules no longer derive them. The first is MTCH's `0000891103@2020-08-10`: the July 2020 IAC/Match separation is a successor, since spin-offs are not stored (#473) and one series across it would book the spin-off as a ~60% fake loss (owner, #828; option (b) on #922). A row of a listed security, in either table, is never proposed: `master-retract` leaves it out of the row count, the digest and the `[traded]` check, and lists it as kept with the reason (`[kept: master.keep_successors]`, and a count in the summary). An EDGAR ingest's check does not record it, so `underived_master_rows` does not fail on it from the next check (an ingest or an apply) on. A check recorded before the id was listed is read as it was recorded, so health at a T before that check is unchanged. The keep holds only while the build derives no `securities` row for the id: once the rules derive the successor again, its stale rows are judged like any other, and the summary names the id as derived again. The summary also names a listed id whose CIK is not judged this run, and one with no underived row (a typo). A malformed id (not a ten-digit CIK, `@`, and a valid day) refuses the config, so every command fails rather than keep nothing silently.

*Afterwards.* Bars filed under a retracted id are not touched here. `repair-resolution` (#819) deletes them once its resolver no longer assigns them. `ingest --backfill --fill-holes` (#831) then fetches the corrected security's missing bars.

*Known limit.* A row is judged by key against one build. A source that answers only part of the history without recording a failure would make valid `filing` rows look underived. The dry-run list, its digest and the owner's review of both are the check, as for `repair-resolution`.

**Amendment 2026-10-11 (#1506, [ADR 0021](../decisions/0021-alpaca-assets-borrow-status-record.md)): the borrow-status record, and the Alpaca spin-off and name-change actions.** Two small ingests from Alpaca into the point-in-time store, from the source inventory's items 3 and 4 (owner direction 2026-10-11, #1506). Neither opens shorting, and neither moves any stored `known_at`. **What does change, stated plainly:** (i) no backtest, trial, sweep or risk rule reads the new rows, and the data vintage is defined so that writing them stales no trial (the vintage rule below); (ii) the paper path's `window.start` already reads `spinoff` rows in the T64 convention and today never finds one, so with real rows it will accept a spin-off child position it refused before and journal a `spinoff_receipt` that `exits.py` then sells whole; that is a behaviour change on the paper path, the `window.py` docstring asks the owner to confirm the convention before a real spin-off relies on it, and **open question 19 is that confirmation**; (iii) `repair-resolution` gains a rule for `spinoff` rows. The borrow record is [ADR 0021](../decisions/0021-alpaca-assets-borrow-status-record.md)'s decision (a new fact table from an already-decided source, under the research-program rule); the spin-off and name-change rows need no decision record, since [ADR 0009](../decisions/0009-price-vendor.md) already covers Alpaca's corporate-actions endpoint and the adapter already fetches these categories and drops them (`parse_corporate_actions`'s `unsupported` list). The code is plan tasks T171 to T171e (borrow) and T172 to T172e (actions), one PR each; this amendment is the class-B docs PR for both.

*The vintage rule (both tables).* `registry.data_vintage` reads an **explicit list of the tables and rows a trial can read**: every `TABLE_PROVENANCE_VALUES` table except `borrow_status` and `symbol_changes`, and of `corporate_actions` only the rows whose `action_type` is `split` or `dividend`. `registry.store_max_ingested_at` stays whole: it is the store's state, not a trial's, and its other callers need every table (the backup-equality check when a data release opens must see a borrow snapshot the backup lacks; a trial registered before vintages exist compares against it and is staled by the new rows, which is accepted for those few). The strategy-lab spec's Definitions sentence on the vintage ("over fact-table rows") is narrowed by this rule and by #1498's `rates`; one cross-reference clause is added to that sentence by whichever of the two docs PRs lands second, since #1499 edits the sentence. Without this, T172e's backfill (six years of rows stamped from 2020-06 with `ingested_at` = the run) would move the vintage of every trial with a cutoff after mid-2020 and fail every open sweep with `STORE_CHANGED`, and the daily borrow snapshot would do the same for every cutoff after 2026-10-11, although no trial reads a row of either. A test pins it: adding a `borrow_status` row, a `symbol_changes` row and a `spinoff` row to the fixture store changes `data_vintage` at no cutoff, and a bar still does. #1498's T169b makes the same change for `rates`, so the list is one place and whichever lands later merges `main` first. The dashboard header's "as of" and "last updated" reads (`dashboard/header.py`) leave `borrow_status` and the `alpaca_assets` source out for the same reason: a morning snapshot must not show the store fresher than its bars.

*Borrow status: the table.* `borrow_status(asset_id, symbol, exchange, status, tradable, marginable, shortable, easy_to_borrow, borrow_status, fractionable, present)` plus the four common columns; `source = alpaca`, `provenance = snapshot` only (Definitions: a current-only endpoint, `known_at` = fetch time). `asset_id` is Alpaca's asset UUID (`symbol` is the symbol on the fetch day; the id is assumed stable across a rename, and nothing rests on it: if it is not, the diff records the old id as vanished and the new one as new, which is also the truth), `exchange` and `status` the payload's strings verbatim (`active`, `inactive`), the five flags booleans, `borrow_status` the payload's string verbatim (`easy_to_borrow`, `hard_to_borrow`, ...; the empty string when the field is absent, never NULL), `present` TRUE on every row written from a payload and FALSE on the one row written when an asset seen before is missing from a snapshot (the other columns carried from its last row; it reappears with a normal row). **UNIQUE `(asset_id, known_at)`.** Only `class = us_equity` assets are rows; every other class is counted (`borrow_other_class`). A symbol can belong to several `us_equity` assets (the recorder's first file: 252 symbols with more than one asset, mostly one active beside inactive ones), which is why the key is the id. No `security_id` column: the asset's symbol on its fetch day maps to a security through the store resolver at that session, as action rows do, and the first consumer adds that read when one exists (none does; ADR 0021 point 4), so a listing repair never forces a borrow re-ingest. Schema version bump with an additive migration (the table is new; no existing row changes); the table joins `TABLE_NAMES` and `TABLE_PROVENANCE_VALUES`, so the look-ahead harness, `health`'s common rules and `export` see it as they see every fact table, and the vintage rule above leaves it out.

*Borrow status: the stamp.* `known_at` is the run's clock read **after** the response arrived (for an imported file, its recorded `fetch_finished_utc`); never the request start, never a date, never back-dated. `BorrowStatus` (the record) carries `fetched_at` and refuses a `known_at` before it. `known_at ≤ ingested_at` holds because the write reads `ingested_at` **after** the response (the bars path reads it before its fetch, `ingest.py`; the borrow path may not), and an import stamps the import run's clock; the test's fake clock is strictly increasing so the wrong order is caught. A snapshot whose fetch time is after the run's clock, or an imported file whose `fetch_finished_utc` precedes its `fetch_started_utc`, is refused and counted, never clamped. History starts at the first recording, 2026-10-11 08:09:54Z; no row is ever written for a day before the snapshot that showed it (ADR 0021 point 6).
